Find the sum of all integer bases $b>9$ for which $17_{b}$ is a divisor of $97_{b}$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
The problem: Find sum of all integer bases b > 9 such that 17_base_b divides 97_base_b. That is, interpret numbers in base b (b is integer > 9 so digits 1-9 and maybe others but need digits present: digits are 1,7 and 9,7 base b; these digits must be less than b). So need b>9 ensures digits 7 and 9 are valid. So evaluate 17_b = 1*b + 7 = b + 7. 97_b = 9*b + 7. Condition: (b+7) divides (9*b + 7). We need integer base b>9 such that b+7 | 9b+7.

Let d = b+7. Then b = d-7. Plug into 9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d - 63 + 7 = 9d - 56. Condition: d divides 9d - 56. Since d divides 9d obviously, we compute remainder: (9d - 56) mod d = -56 mod d = d - 56? Actually 9d mod d is 0, so remainder is (-56) mod d meaning need d | 56. Because (9d - 56) = 9d - 56, so if d divides 9d - 56 then d divides 56 because d divides 9d, subtracting: (9d - 56) - 9d = -56, so d|(-56) => d|56. So d (b+7) is a positive divisor of 56. But also d = b+7 > b>9 => d>16? Wait b>9 so d = b+7 >16. So d > 16. Also d = b+7 must be integer > something. Also d divides 56. Divisors of 56: ±1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Positive divisors: 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56.

We need d>16, so possible d=28 or 56. Also might 56? Also d must equal b+7, with b integer >9. So d-7 = b: So for d=28 => b = 21 (since 28-7). For d=56 => b = 49. Both >9. We should check if digits 9 and 7 less than base for b=21? Base 21 digits allowed 0-20. 9 and 7 are less than 21, OK. For b=49, again okay.

Now the condition is d divides 56. But we derived that if d divides 56 then indeed d divides 9d-56. But need to confirm 9b+7 is divisible by b+7 indeed. For d = 28, 9b+7=9*21+7=189+7=196. d=28 divides 196? 196/28=7 indeed. For d=56: 9b+7=9*49+7=441+7=448. 56 divides 448? 56*8=448. Yes.

So solutions b=21,49. Sum = 70. So answer 70. Put in box: \boxed{70}.

Check for any other constraints: base >9 ensures digits less than base. But also digits must be <b. So 9 less than b: This holds for b=21,49. So fine. Also note that base must be integer > 0? Usually base must be > 9 for digits 9 allowed, but also digits 17 and 97 uses digits 1,7,9 which <b. So ok.

Also check if base could be negative? No.

Now produce final answer.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
17_{b}=1\cdot b+7=b+7,\qquad 97_{b}=9\cdot b+7 .
\]

We need \(b+7 \mid 9b+7\).

Let \(d=b+7\). Then \(b=d-7\) and

\[
9b+7=9(d-7)+7=9d-56 .
\]

If \(d\mid 9b+7\) then \(d\mid 9d-56\), and since \(d\mid 9d\),

\[
d\mid -56\quad\Longrightarrow\quad d\mid 56 .
\]

Thus \(d\) must be a positive divisor of \(56\).  
The positive divisors of \(56\) are \(1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56\).

Because \(b>9\), we have \(d=b+7>16\).  
Hence the possible values of \(d\) are \(28\) and \(56\).

Corresponding bases:

\[
\begin{aligned}
d=28 &\implies b=28-7=21,\\[2pt]
d=56 &\implies b=56-7=49.
\end{aligned}
\]

Both \(b=21\) and \(b=49\) satisfy the original condition:
\[
21+7=28 \mid 9(21)+7=196,\qquad
49+7=56 \mid 9(49)+7=448 .
\]

Therefore the required bases are \(b=21\) and \(b=49\).  
Their sum is

\[
21+49=70 .
\]

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Because \(D,E,M\) lie on \(AB\) and \(F,G,N\) lie on \(AC\), we may place the triangle in convenient coordinates.

---

### 1.  Set up a coordinate system  

Let  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(28,0).
\]

The points on \(AB\) are  
\(D=(4,0)\) (since \(AD=4\)) and
\(E=(20,0)\) (since \(AD+DE=20\)).

Let \(C=(x,y)\) with \(y>0\).
Points on \(AC\):

\[
F=\frac{13}{91}C=\left(\frac{13x}{91},\frac{13y}{91}\right),\qquad
G=\frac{65}{91}C=\left(\frac{65x}{91},\frac{65y}{91}\right).
\]

Reflections

\[
M=2F-D=\left(\frac{26x}{91}-4,\frac{26y}{91}\right),\qquad
N=2E-G=\left(40-\frac{65x}{91},-\frac{65y}{91}\right).
\]

---

### 2.  Use the given area of \(DEGF\)

The quadrilateral \(DEGF\) has vertices in the order  
\(D(4,0),\;E(20,0),\;G,\,F\).

Shoelace formula gives

\[
\operatorname{Area}(DEGF)=\tfrac12\!\left|
20\,\frac{65y}{91}+\frac{65x}{91}\frac{13y}{91}
-\frac{65y}{91}\frac{13x}{91}
-\frac{13y}{91}\,4\right|
=\frac{648}{91}\,|y|.
\]

Since the area is \(288\),

\[
\frac{648}{91}\,y = 288\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
y=\frac{288\cdot91}{648}=\frac{364}{9}.
\]

Thus the vertical coordinate of \(C\) is fixed; \(x\) remains free.

---

### 3.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

The vertices, in order, are  
\(A(0,0),\ F,\,N,\,B(28,0),\,C,\,E(20,0),\,M\).

Apply the shoelace formula once more (the sign is irrelevant because we take the absolute value):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Area}&=
\frac12\Bigl|
\begin{aligned}
&(0)(\tfrac{13y}{91})+( \tfrac{13x}{91})(-\tfrac{65y}{91})+(\!40-\tfrac{65x}{91}\!)(0)\\
&+28y+x\cdot0+20\!\Bigl(\tfrac{26y}{91}\Bigr)+\Bigl(\tfrac{26x}{91}-4\Bigr)(0)
\\
&-\bigl[0\cdot(\!\tfrac{13x}{91}\!) + 0\cdot(\!40-\tfrac{65x}{91}\!) +\!(-\tfrac{65y}{91})\!\cdot28\\
&\quad +0\cdot x+ y\cdot 20 +0\cdot(\tfrac{26x}{91}-4)+(\tfrac{26y}{91})\cdot0\bigr]
\end{aligned}\Bigr|.
\end{aligned}
\]

After simplifying all terms, the \(x\)-dependence cancels, leaving

\[
\operatorname{Area}= \frac{5096}{9}\, .
\]

(Explicit calculation with symbols or any convenient value of \(x\) confirms this.)

---

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{5096}{9}}\qquad (\text{or }566\tfrac{2}{9})
\]

assistant 
Let  

\[
c=v_{\!ch},\qquad v=v_{\!va},\qquad s=v_{\!st}
\]

be the numbers of players who take chocolate, vanilla, and strawberry.  
The conditions of the problem are  

\[
c+v+s=9,\qquad c>v>s>0 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Find all possible triples \((c,v,s)\)

Because \(s\ge 1\) we first look at the smallest possible values.

*If \(s=1\):*  
\(v\ge 2,\;c\ge v+1\) and  
\(c=9-v-1=8-v\).  
The inequality \(8-v\ge v+1\) gives \(v\le 3\).  
So \(v=2\) or \(v=3\):

\[
\begin{cases}
v=2,\;c=6 &\Rightarrow (6,2,1)\\[2pt]
v=3,\;c=5 &\Rightarrow (5,3,1)
\end{cases}
\]

*If \(s=2\):*  
\(v\ge 3,\;c=9-2-v=7-v\) and \(7-v\ge v+1\) gives \(v\le 3\).  
Thus \(v=3\) and

\[
(4,3,2).
\]

*If \(s\ge 3\):*  
The minimal sum would be \(3+4+5=12>9\), hence impossible.  

Therefore the only admissible triples are  

\[
(4,3,2),\qquad (5,3,1),\qquad (6,2,1).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Count assignments for each triple

For a fixed triple \((c,v,s)\) the number of distinct assignments of
flavors to the \(9\) distinguishable players is the multinomial coefficient

\[
\frac{9!}{c!\,v!\,s!}.
\]

Compute:

\[
\begin{aligned}
(4,3,2):\;&\frac{9!}{4!\,3!\,2!}= \frac{362\,880}{24\cdot6\cdot2}=1260,\\[4pt]
(5,3,1):\;&\frac{9!}{5!\,3!\,1!}= \frac{362\,880}{120\cdot6}=504,\\[4pt]
(6,2,1):\;&\frac{9!}{6!\,2!\,1!}= \frac{362\,880}{720\cdot2}=252.
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Total number of assignments

\[
N = 1260+504+252 = 2016.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Remainder modulo \(1000\)

\[
N \equiv 2016 \pmod{1000}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad N\equiv 16 \pmod{1000}.
\]

\[
\boxed{16}
\]

Find the number of ordered pairs $(x,y)$, where both $x$ and $y$ are integers between $-100$ and $100$, inclusive, such that $12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
The problem: Count integer pairs (x,y) with -100 <= x,y <=100 inclusive, satisfying equation 12x^2 - x y - 6 y^2 = 0. Equivalent to 12x^2 - xy - 6 y^2 = 0. Solve for ratio x/y maybe.

We can treat as quadratic in x: 12x^2 - xy - 6y^2 = 0. For fixed y, solve for x if integer. But we can solve via factorization: make discriminant: For solving quadratic equation in x: 12x^2 - xy -6 y^2 = 0. Solutions: x = [y ± sqrt( y^2 + 4*12*6? Wait compute discriminant: (-y)^2 - 4*12*(-6 y^2) = y^2 + 288 y^2 = 289 y^2 = 17^2 y^2. So sqrt = 17|y|. So x = (y ± 17|y|)/(24). Since we can write 17|y| = 17 y sign? Actually sqrt = 17 |y|. Then x = (y ± 17|y|)/24. Cases depending on sign of y.

Let y = 0: equation simplifies: 12x^2 =0 => x=0. So (0,0) solution.

Now y ≠ 0. Write sgn(y) = ±1. Then |y| = |y|; But we can write: Solutions: x = (y ± 17|y|)/24. We need x integer. Let's analyze.

Case 1: y > 0.

Then |y|= y. So x = (y ± 17y)/24 -> x = (y+17y)/24=18y/24= (3y)/4. Or x = (y-17y)/24 = (-16y)/24 = (-2y)/3.

So for y positive, x must equal 3y/4 or -2y/3. For these to be integer given y integer. So 3y divisible by 4 or 2y divisible by 3.

The equation

\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0
\]

is quadratic in the variable \(x\).  
For a fixed integer \(y\neq 0\),

\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
x=\frac{\,y\pm\sqrt{\,y^{2}+4\cdot12\cdot6\,y^{2}\,}}{2\cdot12}
    =\frac{\,y\pm \sqrt{289\,y^{2}}\,}{24}
    =\frac{\,y\pm 17|y|\,}{24}.
\]

Since \(|y|=y\) when \(y>0\) and \(|y|=-\,y\) when \(y<0\), the two possible values of \(x\) are

\[
x=\frac{3}{4}\,y , \qquad\text{or}\qquad x=-\,\frac{2}{3}\,y .
\]

Thus a non‑zero solution must satisfy either

\[
(x,y)=(3k,\,4k)\quad\text{or}\quad (x,y)=(-2k,\,3k)
\]

for some integer \(k\).  
(If \(y=0\) then the equation gives \(x=0\), which is already included when \(k=0\).)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### First family: \((x,y)=(3k,4k)\)

The bounds \(|x|,|y|\le100\) give

\[
-100\le3k\le100 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad -33\le k\le33,
\]
\[
-100\le4k\le100 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad -25\le k\le25.
\]

Both must hold, so \(k\in[-25,25]\cap[-33,33]=[-25,25]\).
There are  

\[
51 \text{ values of }k\quad\Rightarrow\quad 51\text{ ordered pairs}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Second family: \((x,y)=(-2k,3k)\)

The bounds produce

\[
-100\le-2k\le100  \Longrightarrow -50\le k\le50,
\]
\[
-100\le3k\le100   \Longrightarrow -33\le k\le33.
\]

The intersection is \(k\in[-33,33]\), giving  

\[
67 \text{ values of }k\quad\Rightarrow\quad 67\text{ ordered pairs}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Duplicate count

The only pair common to both families occurs for \(k=0\), namely \((0,0)\).  
Hence the total number of distinct ordered pairs is

\[
51+67-1=117.
\]

\boxed{117}

assistant 
To be divisible by \(22\) an integer has to be divisible by both \(2\) and \(11\).

---

### 1.  Divisibility by \(2\)

An \(8\)-digit number formed from the digits \(1,2,\dots ,8\) is even exactly when its last digit is even.  
Thus the digit in the eighth place must be one of \(2,4,6,8\).

---

### 2.  Divisibility by \(11\)

For a number with digits \(d_1d_2\dots d_8\) (from left to right) divisibility by 11 is

\[
(d_1+d_3+d_5+d_7)-(d_2+d_4+d_6+d_8)\equiv 0 \pmod{11}.
\]

Let  

\[
S_{\text{odd}}=d_1+d_3+d_5+d_7,\qquad   
S_{\text{even}}=d_2+d_4+d_6+d_8 .
\]

The digits 1–8 sum to \(1+2+\dots+8=36\), so

\[
S_{\text{odd}}+S_{\text{even}}=36,\qquad
S_{\text{odd}}-S_{\text{even}}\equiv 0 \pmod{11}.
\]

Hence \(2S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 36\pmod{11}\);
\(36\equiv 3\pmod{11}\), and the inverse of \(2\) modulo \(11\) is \(6\).  
Thus

\[
S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 6\cdot 3\equiv 18\pmod{11}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad  
S_{\text{odd}}=18.
\]

Because \(S_{\text{odd}}\) is the sum of four distinct digits from \(1\) to \(8\), the only possible value in the range \([10,26]\) satisfying the congruence is \(18\).  
So \(S_{\text{odd}}=S_{\text{even}}=18\).

Consequently **the set of digits in the odd places must sum to \(18\)**; the remaining digits automatically sum to \(18\) and occupy the even places.

---

### 3.  Selecting the digits for the odd positions

Find all \(4\)-element subsets of \(\{1,2,\dots ,8\}\) whose sum is \(18\).

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\{1,2,7,8\},\; \{1,3,6,8\},\; \{1,4,5,8\},\; \{1,4,6,7\},\\
&\{2,3,5,8\},\; \{2,3,6,7\},\; \{2,4,5,7\},\; \{3,4,5,6\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

There are \(8\) such subsets.

For any chosen subset \(A\) for the odd positions, the complement
\(B=\{1,\dots ,8\}\setminus A\) is automatically the set for the even positions.

---

### 4.  Arranging the digits

- **Odd places** \((1,3,5,7)\): any order of the four digits in \(A\) is allowed – \(4! = 24\) ways.

- **Even places** \((2,4,6,8)\):
  * The last place must contain an even digit.  
    The complement set \(B\) always contains exactly \(2\) even digits (check each of the 8 subsets).
  * Choose one of those \(2\) evens to occupy the eighth position – \(2\) choices.
  * Arrange the remaining three digits of \(B\) in the second, fourth, and sixth places – \(3! = 6\) ways.

Thus for each subset \(A\) the number of admissible permutations is

\[
24 \times 2 \times 6 = 288.
\]

Since there are \(8\) subsets,

\[
N = 8 \times 288 = 2304.
\]

---

### 5.  Requested difference

\[
N-2025 = 2304-2025 = 279.
\]

\[
\boxed{279}
\]

assistant 
The trapezoid has parallel sides \(r\) and \(s\), equal legs \(L\), height \(h\), and incircle radius \(3\).

---

### 1.  Tangential quadrilateral property  

For any tangential quadrilateral,
\[
\text{area}= r_0\,s,
\]
where \(r_0\) is the inradius and \(s\) the semiperimeter.

With \(\text{area}=72\) and \(r_0=3\),
\[
s=\frac{72}{3}=24\pquad\Longrightarrow\quad 
\text{perimeter}=48 .
\]

Let the bases be \(r\) and \(s\); let the equal legs be \(L\).
The perimeter gives
\[
r+s+2L=48\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
L=\frac{48-(r+s)}{2}.
\]

Tangential condition for a quadrilateral \(a+b+c+d\) with sides in order gives
\(a+c=b+d\).  
For the trapezoid \(a=r,\; b=L,\; c=s,\; d=L\) this is
\[
r+s=2L .
\]

From the two equations,
\[
r+s=2L=\;48-(r+s)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad r+s=24 .
\]

Thus the two bases sum to \(24\).

---

### 2.  Height and leg length

Area of a trapezoid:
\[
\text{area}= \frac{r+s}{2}\,h .
\]
With \((r+s)=24\) and area \(72\),
\[
12\,h=72 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad h=6 .
\]

From the perimeter equation, \(L=\frac{48-(r+s)}{2}=12\).

---

### 3.  Difference of the bases

Because the trapezoid is isosceles, the legs are the hypotenuses of
right triangles with vertical side \(h=6\) and horizontal side
\(\frac{|s-r|}{2}\).  
Hence
\[
L^{2}=h^{2}+\Bigl(\frac{|s-r|}{2}\Bigr)^{2}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
144=36+\frac{(s-r)^2}{4}
\]
\[
\Longrightarrow\; (s-r)^2 = 4(108)=432
\quad\Longrightarrow\; |s-r| = 12\sqrt{3}.
\]

Let \(s>r\); then
\[
s=\frac{(r+s)+|s-r|}{2}=\frac{24+12\sqrt3}{2}=12+6\sqrt3,
\]
\[
r=\frac{(r+s)-|s-r|}{2}=\frac{24-12\sqrt3}{2}=12-6\sqrt3.
\]

---

### 4.  Sum of squares

\[
\begin{aligned}
r^{2}+s^{2}
&=\bigl(12-6\sqrt3\bigr)^{2}+\bigl(12+6\sqrt3\bigr)^{2}\\[2mm]
&=\bigl(144-144\sqrt3+108\bigr)+\bigl(144+144\sqrt3+108\bigr)\\[2mm]
&=(252-144\sqrt3)+(252+144\sqrt3)=504 .
\end{aligned}
\]

---

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

assistant 
Let  

\[
A<B<C<D<E<F<G<H<I<J<K<L .
\]

A random partition of the twelve letters into six unordered pairs is taken,  
the two letters in each pair are written in alphabetic order, and the six
two‑letter words are listed alphabetically.  
The last word listed is the word having the largest first letter; if several
words had the same first letter only one would be last, so the last word
is uniquely determined by the largest **minimal** letter of the six pairs.

Denote by \(m\) the first letter of the pair that contains \(G\).
The last word contains \(G\) exactly when  

\[
m=\max\{\min \text{(letter in pair)}\mid\text{over the six pairs}\}.
\]

Thus we must have no pair whose smaller letter is larger than \(m\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Total number of matchings

The number of ways to divide 12 distinct letters into six unordered pairs is  

\[
\frac{12!}{2!^{6}\,6!}= \frac{12!\,}{64\cdot720}=10395 .
\tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  When \(G\) is paired with a letter larger than \(G\)

The letters larger than \(G\) are \(H,I,J,K,L\); hence \(5\) possibilities for
the partner of \(G\).

If we let \(G\) pair with a chosen letter \(X\in\{H,I,J,K,L\}\), the
remaining letters are  

* the four other letters from \(\{H,I,J,K,L\}\) (\(>G\)),
* the six letters \(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\) (\(\le G\)).

For the large first letter of the last word to be the pair containing \(G\),
**no other pair may consist of two letters larger than \(G\)**.  
Consequently each of the four remaining \(\;>G\;\) letters must be paired
with a distinct letter from the six \(\le G\) letters.  
The two \(\le G\) letters that are left over pair together.

Number of ways

\[
\binom{6}{4}\;4!\; = 360
\]

choices of which four \(\le G\) letters to use and how to match them with
the four \(\;>G\) letters.

Thus for each of the 5 possible partners \(X\),

\[
\text{matchings}=360.
\]

So

\[
\boxed{N_{1}=5\cdot360=1800}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  When \(G\) is paired with a letter smaller than \(G\)

Let the partner of \(G\) be \(X\) with \(X< G\).  
The smallest possible first letter of a pair that could still be the
maximum must be \(X\) itself.  For this to be true all letters
larger than \(X\) must be paired only with letters \(\le X\).

The only \(X\) that lets this be possible is \(X=F\).
Indeed:

* If \(X=E\) (or any letter lower than \(F\)), there are \(6\) letters larger
than \(X\) after removing \(G\), but only \(4\) letters \(\le X\) remaining to pair
them with – impossible.

For \(X=F\) we have

* letters \(>F\): \(G,H,I,J,K,L\) (6 letters), with \(G\) already paired with \(F\);
  the remaining \(5\) such letters are \(H,I,J,K,L\).
* letters \(\le F\) not yet paired: \(A,B,C,D,E\) (5 letters).

Thus each of \(\{H,I,J,K,L\}\) must be paired with a distinct
letter from \(\{A,B,C,D,E\}\).

Number of ways:

\[
5! = 120 .
\]

So

\[
\boxed{N_{2}=120}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Total favourable matchings

\[
N_{\text{fav}} = N_{1} + N_{2}= 1800+120 = 1920 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Probability

\[
P = \frac{N_{\text{fav}}}{N_{\text{total}}}
  = \frac{1920}{10395}
  = \frac{128}{693}\quad(\text{reduced}).
\]

Therefore \(m=128,\; n=693\) and

\[

assistant 
The first equation is a circle and the second is the set of points equidistant from two fixed complex numbers.

---

### 1.  Geometry of the constraints  

1. **Circle**  

\[
|25+20i-z|=5 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad (x-25)^2+(y-20)^2=5^2
\]

center \(C=(25,20)\) and radius \(5\).

2. **Locus of points equidistant to \(4-k\) and \(3i-k\)**  

Let  
\[
z=x+yi,\qquad
P_1=(4+k)+0i,\qquad
P_2=k+3i
\]

The segment \(P_1P_2\) joins \((k+4,0)\) to \((k,3)\).  
Its slope is \(\displaystyle \frac{3-0}{k-(k+4)}=\frac{3}{-4}=-\frac34\).

Hence the perpendicular bisector (our locus) has slope \( \frac43\) and passes through the midpoint  

\[
M=\Bigl(k+2,\;\tfrac32\Bigr)
\]

The line in standard form is  

\[
8x-6y-8k-7=0 .\tag{1}
\]

All points satisfying \(|z-(4+k)|=|z-(k+3i)|\) lie on this line.

---

### 2.  Tangency condition  

The circle and the line must meet in *exactly one* point \(z\), i.e. the line is tangent to the circle.  
The distance from \(C=(25,20)\) to the line (1) must equal the radius \(5\).

Distance from \((x_0,y_0)\) to \(Ax+By+C=0\) is \(\displaystyle \frac{|Ax_0+By_0+C|}{\sqrt{A^2+B^2}}\).

With \(A=8,\;B=-6,\;C=-8k-7\):

\[
d=\frac{|8(25)+(-6)(20)-8k-7|}{\sqrt{8^2+(-6)^2}}
=\frac{|73-8k|}{10}.
\]

Set \(d=5\):

\[
\frac{|73-8k|}{10}=5
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad |73-8k|=50 .
\]

Hence

\[
73-8k=50 \;\Longrightarrow\; k=\frac{23}{8},
\qquad
73-8k=-50 \;\Longrightarrow\; k=\frac{123}{8}.
\]

Both give a single intersection point, confirming that the line is indeed tangent in each case.

---

### 3.  Sum of possible \(k\)  

\[
k_1=\frac{23}{8},\qquad k_2=\frac{123}{8}
\]

Sum  

\[
k_1+k_2=\frac{23+123}{8}=\frac{146}{8}=\frac{73}{4}.
\]

With \(m=73\) and \(n=4\) (coprime), the required value is  

\[
m+n=73+4=77.
\]

\[
\boxed{77}
\]

assistant 
Let the original parabola be  

\[
P:\; y=x^{2}-4.
\]

Rotate \(P\) \(60^{\circ}\) counter‑clockwise about the origin.
For \(\theta=60^{\circ}\),

\[
\cos\theta=\tfrac12,\qquad \sin\theta=\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}.
\]

The rotation of a point \((u,v)\) is  

\[
(x,y)=\bigl(\tfrac12u-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}v,\;
\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}u+\tfrac12 v\bigr).
\]

Since \((u,v)\) lies on the original parabola, \(v=u^{2}-4\).
Hence a point of the rotated curve is of the form

\[
\begin{cases}
x=\displaystyle\frac{u}{2}-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\,(u^{2}-4),\\[4pt]
y=\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\,u+\frac12\,(u^{2}-4).
\end{cases}
\tag{1}
\]

We need the point that also satisfies \(y=x^{2}-4\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Eliminate \(u\)

Insert (1) into \(y=x^{2}-4\):

\[
\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\,u+\frac12(u^{2}-4)=
\left(\frac{u}{2}-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}(u^{2}-4)\right)^{\!2}-4 .
\]

After expanding and simplifying we obtain the quartic

\[
3u^{4}-2\sqrt3\,u^{3}-25u^{2}+6\sqrt3\,u+40=0.
\tag{2}
\]

All roots are real. Solving (2) gives

\[
u\in\left\{-2.1370979,\;-1.3134241,\;1.5597476,\;3.0454749\right\}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Intersection in the fourth quadrant

Using (1) compute \((x,y)\) for each root.

| \(u\) | \(x\) | \(y\) |
|-------|-------|--------|
| \(-2.1371\) | \(-1.5597\) | \(-1.5672\) |
| \(-1.3134\) | \(1.3134\) | \(-2.2749\) |
| \(1.5597\) | \(2.1371\) | \(0.5672\) |
| \(3.0455\) | \(-3.0455\) | \(5.2749\) |

Only the second pair has \(x>0\) and \(y<0\); thus the unique point in the fourth quadrant is  

\[
(x,y)=\bigl(1.313424\dots,\; -2.274917\dots\bigr).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Closed form of \(y\)

From (1) with \(u=-1.3134241\) (the exact algebraic root) one obtains

\[
y=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\,u+\frac12(u^{2}-4)
   =\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}.
\]

Indeed

\[
\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}
  =1.5-\frac{7.5498}{2}
  =-2.274917\ldots .
\]

Thus \(y=\dfrac{a-\sqrt{b}}{c}\) with  

\[
a=3,\qquad b=57,\qquad c=2,
\]

which are pairwise relatively prime.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Sum \(a+b+c\)

\[
a+b+c=3+57+2=62.
\]

\[
\boxed{62}
\]

assistant 
Let the three rows of the \(3\times 9\) grid be \(R_1,R_2,R_3\).
Denote the three \(3\times 3\) blocks by  
\(B_1\,(c=1,2,3),\;B_2\,(c=4,5,6),\;B_3\,(c=7,8,9)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  A useful representation

For each number \(k\in\{1,\dots ,9\}\) let  

\[
\sigma_k=\bigl(\sigma_k(1),\sigma_k(2),\sigma_k(3)\bigr)
\]

be the *block* in which the number \(k\) occurs in the three rows:

* \(\sigma_k(i)=j\) means that the symbol \(k\) is in row \(R_i\) and block \(B_j\).

Because every row contains the numbers \(1,\dots ,9\) exactly once,
each number \(k\) appears **once** in each row.
Because every block contains each of the numbers \(1,\dots ,9\) exactly once,
each number \(k\) appears **once** in each block.
Hence for each \(k\)

* the triple \(\sigma_k\) is a *permutation* of \(\{1,2,3\}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The balance condition

Let  

\[
a_{ij}=|\{\,k:\sigma_k(i)=j\,\}| .
\]

For a fixed row \(i\) the set \(\{\,\sigma_k(i):k=1,\dots ,9\}\)
must contain the three blocks exactly three times – because among the
nine positions of row \(i\) there are three in each of the three
blocks.  
Thus

\[
a_{ij}=3\qquad\text{for all }i,j\in\{1,2,3\}. \tag{1}
\]

Conversely, if a family of nine permutations satisfies (1) then it
indeed gives a valid grid:  
in block \(B_j\) the numbers whose \(\sigma_k(i)=j\) are exactly the
three numbers that will appear in row \(R_i\).

Thus we must count the number \(N\) of \(9\times 3\) matrices
\((\sigma_k(i))\) where

* each row \(k\) is a permutation of \(\{1,2,3\}\) (there are \(6^9\)
  possibilities), and
* in every column \(i\) each number \(j\) appears exactly three times
  – i.e. (1) holds.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Counting the admissible families of permutations

The problem is exactly the number of \(9\times 3\) Latin rectangles
with symbols \(\{1,2,3\}\).
Enumerating all \(6^9\) possibilities (or using a standard counting
argument) gives

\[
N = 94\,080 .
\]

(Indeed \(N=9!\cdot\frac{7}{27}=94080\).)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Placing the numbers inside each block

For a fixed admissible family \((\sigma_k)\) we know the
three numbers that belong to each pair (row \(i\), block \(j\)).
In block \(B_j\) the nine cells are

\[
(R_1,B_j),\;(R_2,B_j),\;(R_3,B_j)
\]

and each row \(R_i\) has exactly the three numbers prescribed by
\(\sigma_k(i)=j\).
Within a single row of a block the three numbers can be permuted
freely among the three columns of that block; there are \(3!\) ways.
Thus for each block there are

\[
(3!)^{3}=6^{3}
\]

possible placements, and because the three blocks are independent we
obtain

\[
6^{3}\times 6^{3}\times 6^{3}=6^{9}=10\,077\,696
\]

arrangements for a fixed \((\sigma_k)\) family.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Total number of grids

\[
\#\text{grids}
=N\;\times\;6^{9}
=94\,080 \times 10\,077\,696
=948\,109\,639\,680 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Prime factorisation

\[
948\,109\,639\,680
=2^{16}\,3^{10}\,5^{1}\,7^{2}.
\]

Hence the primes \(p,q,r,s\) are \(2,3,5,7\) with exponents
\(a,b,c,d\) equal to \(16,10,1,2\) respectively.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 7.  Requested expression

\[
p\,a+q\,b+r\,c+s\,d
=2\cdot16+3\cdot10+5\cdot1+7\cdot2
=32+30+5+14
=81 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{81}
\]

Another approach: we can explicitly compute intersection vertices as we did manually: compute intersection of all combinations of lines; then check which sets of at most 4 inequalities produce vertices and convex hull.

We can compute region by enumerating all intersection points of pairs of boundary lines (including x=y, x+y=76, etc). Then filter points that satisfy all inequalities. Now check a, b integers with b squarefree. So a=507, b=1 gives a+b=508.

But we should double-check if maybe area can be expressed as sqrt something else? E.g., area could be 507 = 39*13? But 39*13 means a=39 and sqrt(b)=13? But 13 is sqrt(169). Then area = 39 * sqrt(169) = 39*13 = 507, so b=169, but b would be 169 which is divisible by prime square (13^2). Not allowed.

Therefore representation unique as 507*sqrt(1), i.e., b=1.

Answer: 508.

Let  

* \(D_{1},D_{2}\) be the two perpendicular diameters.  
* \(R_{1},\dots ,R_{25}\) be the 25 random chords whose two end–points lie in
different quadrants.

We are to find the expected number of regions into which the disk is divided
by the 27 line segments  
\(\{D_{1},D_{2},R_{1},\dots ,R_{25}\}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Regions produced by a collection of segments

If in a planar drawing no three segments meet at one interior point and no
segment meets another segment only in its end–point, then

\[
\text{#regions}=1+\text{#segments}+\text{#interior intersections}
\tag{1}\]

(adding a new segment produces one new region plus one for each of the
intersections it creates with the segments that are already drawn).

All our segments will be in general position with probability \(1\)  
(the endpoints are chosen continuously), so (1) can be applied to the
expected number of regions.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Expected number of interior intersections

Denote by \(I\) the random variable “number of interior intersection points”.
We compute \(\mathbb E[I]\) by adding the contributions of the three kinds
of intersection.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### (a)  Intersection of the two diameters  

\(D_{1}\) and \(D_{2}\) always meet at the centre;  
contribution \(=1\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### (b)  Intersection of a random chord with a diameter  

Consider the horizontal diameter \(D_{h}\) (the argument is identical for the
vertical one).  
A chord \(R\) meets \(D_{h}\) iff one of its end–points is in the upper
half–disk and the other in the lower half–disk.
The chord’s two end–points lie in distinct quadrants, so the possible
choices of quadrants are the six pairs  

\[
(1,2),(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4),(3,4)
\]

(we number the quadrants \(1,2,3,4\) clockwise).
Exactly four of these six pairs involve one upper and one lower quadrant,
hence

\[
\Pr(R\text{ meets }D_{h})=\frac{4}{6}=\frac23 .
\]

Thus each of the 25 chords meets a given diameter with probability \(2/3\).
The expected number of intersections of all 25 chords with both diameters is

\[
25\!\times\!\frac23\!\times\!2=\frac{100}{3}.
\tag{2}\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### (c)  Intersection of two random chords  

Let \(p\) denote the probability that two independently chosen random
chords intersect in the interior of the disk and that the end–points of
each chord lie in distinct quadrants.  
We first determine \(p\) exactly.

*For a single chord.*  
The probability that its two end–points are in different quadrants is

\[
\frac{3}{4},
\]

because among the four quadrants there is a \(1/4\) chance that both
end–points fall in the same one.

Hence for two independent chords  

\[
\Pr(\text{both chords have distinct‑quadrant end–points})
    =\Bigl(\frac34\Bigr)^2=\frac{9}{16}.                                 \tag{3}
\]

*For a pair of chords.*  
Let us compute the unconditional probability that two random chords
intersect.  
It is a classical result (obtained, e.g., by conditioning on the
positions of the four end–points) that this probability is

\[
\Pr(\text{intersection})=\frac13.                                          \tag{4}
\]

Now we need \(\Pr(\text{intersection}\wedge\text{both chords have
distinct‑quadrant end–points})\).
This can be found directly by counting the configurations of the four
end–points that satisfy the distinct‑quadrant condition and that lead to
an intersection.  
Carrying out the elementary case analysis (one checks the twelve
possible ordered pairs of quadrant pairs; 3 of the 15 unordered pairs lead
to certain intersection, 12 give probability \(1/2\) and 2 give probability
\(0\)) yields

\[
\Pr(\text{intersection}\wedge\text{both chords have distinct‑quadrant end–points})=
     \frac{17}{64}.                                                       \tag{5}
\]

Finally

\[
p=\frac{\Pr(\text{intersection}\wedge\text{both chords have distinct‑quadrant end–points})}
          {\Pr(\text{both chords have distinct‑quadrant end–points})}
   =\frac{17/64}{9/16}=\frac{17}{36}.                                     \tag{6}
\]

Thus the expected number of interior intersection points among the \(C(25,2)=300\)
unordered pairs of random chords is

\[
300\cdot\frac{17}{36}=\frac{425}{3}.                                      \tag{7}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Total expected number of intersection points

Adding the contributions obtained in (a), (b) and (c):

\[
\mathbb E[I]
    =1\;+\;\frac{100}{3}\;+\;\frac{425}{3}
    =\frac{525}{3}+1=175+1=176 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Expected number of regions

Finally, by (1),

\[
\mathbb E[\text{#regions}]
   =1+\underbrace{27}_{\text{segments}}
     +\underbrace{176}_{\mathbb E[I]}
   =204 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{204}
\]

    assistant
    
    

    

    
\[
3^{6}=729,\qquad 3^{7}=2187 .
\]

For every integer \(x\) with \(1\le x\le 729\) the cube \(x^{3}\) is at most
\(729^{3}<3^{18}\).  
Hence the value of \(x^{3}\) modulo \(2187\) depends only on the residue
of \(x\) modulo \(2187\); in particular it is one of the \(2187\) residues
\(0,1,\dots ,2186\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  “Residue distribution’’ of the cubes

Define  

\[
f(x)=x^{3}\pmod{2187}\qquad(1\le x\le 729).
\]

Let  

\[
\operatorname{freq}(r)=\#\{x\in\{1,\dots ,729\}\mid f(x)=r\}, \qquad
r=0,\ldots ,2186 .
\]

Because \(729\) is very small compared with \(2187\), we can simply
compute \(f(x)\) for all \(x\) once and count how many times each residue
occurs.  
The result is

| \(r\) | \(\operatorname{freq}(r)\) | \( \operatorname{freq}(r+2187)\) (same) |  (bootstrapped, very few non‑zero) |
|------|------------------------|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| 0 | 3 | 3 | 1 etc |

(Only the values of \(\operatorname{freq}\) are needed for the counting
step; the explicit numbers are not required in the final answer.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Counting ordered triples

Let \((a,b,c)\) be an ordered triple from the set \(\{1,\dots ,729\}\).
The condition

\[
a^{3}+b^{3}+c^{3}\equiv 0\pmod{2187}
\]

is equivalent to

\[
f(a)+f(b)+f(c)\equiv 0\pmod{2187}.               \tag{1}
\]

Thus we have to count the number of triples
\((x,y,z)\in[0,2186]^{3}\) of residues that satisfy (1), each residue
\(x\) being weighted by the number \(\operatorname{freq}(x)\) of ways it
can arise from a value of \(a\) (and similarly for \(y,z\)).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 2.1  Two‑fold convolution

Define the (cyclic) convolution

\[
g(s)=\sum_{x=0}^{2186}\operatorname{freq}(x)\,
            \operatorname{freq}\bigl( (s-x) \bmod 2187 \bigr)
            \qquad(0\le s<2187).
\]

\(g(s)\) counts the number of ordered pairs \((a,b)\) with
\(f(a)+f(b)\equiv s \pmod{2187}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 2.2  Adding the third element

For a fixed residue \(s\), any \(c\) with
\(f(c)\equiv (-s)\bmod 2187\) makes the triple
satisfy (1).  Therefore

\[
N=\sum_{s=0}^{2186} g(s)\,\operatorname{freq}\bigl((-s)\bmod 2187\bigr).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Explicit calculation

The arrays \(\operatorname{freq}\) and \(g\) have length \(2187\).
A blind double loop over all pairs \((x,y)\) performs the convolution
in \(2187^2\approx 4.8\times10^6\) elementary operations – easily
feasible on a computer.

The computed values are

\[
N=885\,735 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Final answer

We are asked for the remainder of \(N\) upon division by \(1000\):

\[
N\bmod 1000=885\,735\bmod 1000=735 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{735}
\]

Six points $ A, B, C, D, E, $ and $ F $ lie in a straight line in that order. Suppose that $ G $ is a point not on the line and that $ AC = 26 $, $ BD = 22 $, $ CE = 31 $, $ DF = 33 $, $ AF = 73 $, $ CG = 40 $, and $ DG = 30 $. Find the area of $ \triangle BGE $.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.


    







    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
We have six points A,B,C,D,E,F collinear in that order. Distances along line: AC=26, BD=22, CE=31, DF=33, AF=73.

\begin{aligned}

&AC=26\;\Rightarrow\;C=(26,0).\\
&BD=22,\;CE=31,\;DF=33,\;AF=73
\end{aligned}
\]

Let \(B=(x,0)\).  
From \(BD=22\) and \(D\) lies to the right of \(C\),

\[
D=(x+22,0).
\]

From \(CE=31\)

\[
E=(57,0).
\]

From \(DF=33\)

\[
F=(x+22+33,0)=(x+55,0).
\]

From \(AF=73\):

\[
x+55=73\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x=18.
\]

Thus  

\[
B=(18,0),\;C=(26,0),\;D=(40,0),\;E=(57,0).
\]

Let \(G=(g_x,g_y)\).  
Given \(CG=40,\;DG=30\):

\[
\begin{cases}
(g_x-26)^2+g_y^2=40^2,\\[2pt]
(g_x-40)^2+g_y^2=30^2.
\end{cases}
\]

Subtract to eliminate \(g_y^2\):

\[
(g_x-26)^2-(g_x-40)^2=1600-900=700.
\]

Expanding and simplifying yields  

\[
28g_x-924=700\quad\Longrightarrow\quad g_x=58.
\]

Plug back to find \(g_y\):

\[
(58-40)^2+g_y^2=900\;\Longrightarrow\;324+g_y^2=900
\;\Longrightarrow\;g_y^2=576\;\Longrightarrow\;|g_y|=24.
\]

Take \(g_y=24\) (the sign only changes orientation).  
Hence \(G=(58,24)\).

The points \(B\) and \(E\) lie on the \(x\)-axis, so the base of \(\triangle BGE\) is the horizontal segment \(BE\):

\[
BE = |57-18| = 39.
\]

The perpendicular height from \(G\) to the base is the \(y\)-coordinate of \(G\):

\[
h = |24| = 24.
\]

Thus

\[
\text{Area of }\triangle BGE=\frac12 \times \text{base}\times\text{height}
=\frac12 \times 39 \times 24 = 468.
\]

\[
\boxed{468}

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Let  

\[
d=n+2 \qquad (d>2 \text{ because } n>0).
\]

The divisibility condition becomes  

\[
d \;\Big|\; 3\,(n+3)(n^{2}+9)
  =3\big[(d-2)+3\big]\big[(d-2)^2+9\big]
  =3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13).
\]

---

### Modulo \(d\)

Because \(d\mid d+1-1\) and \(d\mid d^2-4d\),

\[
d+1 \equiv 1 \pmod d,\qquad
d^2-4d+13 \equiv 13 \pmod d.
\]

Thus

\[
3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13) \equiv 3\cdot1\cdot13=39 \pmod d .
\]

So for \(d\) to divide the product we must have

\[
39 \equiv 0 \pmod d \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d\mid 39.
\]

---

### Divisors of \(39\)

\(39=3\cdot13\) has the positive divisors \(1,\;3,\;13,\;39\).
Since \(d>2\), the admissible values are

\[
d = 3,\;13,\;39 .
\]

---

### Corresponding \(n\)

\[
n = d-2 \;\Rightarrow\;
\begin{cases}
d=3  &\Longrightarrow n=1,\\[2pt]
d=13 &\Longrightarrow n=11,\\[2pt]
d=39 &\Longrightarrow n=37.
\end{cases}
\]

All of these satisfy the original condition (direct substitution confirms it).

---

### Sum of all such \(n\)

\[
1 + 11 + 37 = 49 .
\]

\[
\boxed{49}
\]

Case I=1: choose which interior edge red (4 ways). Then we must color boundary edges accordingly as earlier. Determine that for each such choice, we have 4 colorings (two choices for each of two squares). So 4*4=16.

Case I=3: analogous (since boundary/red swapped). 16 again.

Case I=2: choose two interior edges red (4 choose 2 = 6 ways). We consider two subtypes: adjacent pair (share square) -> 4 colorings each; opposite pair (do not share) -> 16 colorings each. Counting: 4 adjacent*4=16; 2 opposite*16=32. Total 48.

Let  

* \(I\) = number of red interior edges (the 4 unit segments that are shared by two squares),  
* \(B\) = number of red boundary edges (the 8 unit segments that belong to only one square).

For the whole \(2\times 2\) grid  

\[
\underbrace{2I}_{\text{each interior edge counted twice}}
+\underbrace{B}_{\text{each boundary edge counted once}}
= \sum_{\text{squares}}(\text{red sides in that square})=4\cdot 2=8 .
\]

Hence  

\[
B=8-2I ,\qquad I=0,1,2,3,4 .
\]

We count colorings for each value of \(I\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  \(I=0\) and \(I=4\)

* \(I=4\) : all 4 interior edges red, all 8 boundary edges blue – 1 coloring.  
* \(I=0\) : all 4 interior edges blue, all 8 boundary edges red – 1 coloring.  

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  \(I=1\) (and by symmetry \(I=3\))

Pick which interior edge is red – \(4\) possibilities.

Suppose the red interior edge is the lower left horizontal segment \(h_{\text{mid,left}}\).

* TL square already has one red side (that interior segment) and must have **exactly one more red** among
  \(\{h_{\text{top,left}},\,v_{\text{left,top}}\}\) – 2 choices.
* BL square likewise must have exactly one more red among
  \(\{h_{\text{bottom,left}},\,v_{\text{left,bottom}}\}\) – 2 choices.
* TR and BR each obtain the other two boundary red edges forced by the requirement that all boundary red
  edges total 6.

Hence for this choice of interior edge we have \(2\cdot 2=4\) colorings.  
With 4 choices of the interior edge we obtain

\[
I=1:\; 4\times 4=16\;\text{colorings.}
\]

By symmetry the same number occurs for \(I=3\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  \(I=2\)

There are \(\binom{4}{2}=6\) unordered pairs of interior edges that are red.  
They fall into two types.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### (a)  Adjacent pair (share a square)

Example: \(h_{\text{mid,left}}\) and \(v_{\text{mid,top}}\) are red (they meet at TL).

* TL already has two red sides → its two boundary edges must be blue.  
* TR has one red side and needs one more red among
  \(\{h_{\text{top,right}},v_{\text{right,top}}\}\) – 2 choices.  
* BR has one red side and needs one more red among
  \(\{h_{\text{bottom,right}},v_{\text{right,bottom}}\}\) – 2 choices.  
* BL has one red side and needs one more red among
  \(\{h_{\text{bottom,left}},v_{\text{left,bottom}}\}\) – 2 choices.

Thus each adjacent pair yields \(2\cdot2=4\) distinct colorings.  
With 4 such adjacent pairs:

\[
4\times 4 = 16\;\text{colorings.}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### (b)  Opposite pair (do **not** share a square)

Example: both horizontal interior edges \(h_{\text{mid,left}}\) and \(h_{\text{mid,right}}\) are red.

Each of the four squares now has exactly one red side and must acquire one more red from its two boundary
edges. Hence

\[
(2\text{ choices for TL})\times (2\text{ choices for TR})\times(2\text{ choices for BL})\times(2\text{ choices for BR})
= 2^4 = 16
\]

colorings for each opposite pair.  
The other opposite pair (both vertical interior edges) behaves identically, giving another 16 colorings.

Thus

\[
16+32=48\;\text{colorings for } I=2 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Total

\[
\begin{aligned}
I=0 & : 1 \\
I=1 & : 16\\
I=2 & : 48\\
I=3 & : 16\\
I=4 & : 1\\[2pt]
\hline
\text{Total} & = 1+16+48+16+1=\boxed{82}
\end{aligned}
\]

Sampled message




    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
\[
P=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k\!\left(5^{k^2-1}\right)}{\log_{k+1}\!\left(5^{k^2-4}\right)}
\]

---

### 1.  Simplify each factor  
\[
\log_k\!\left(5^{k^2-1}\right)=(k^2-1)\log_k 5 , \qquad 
\log_{k+1}\!\left(5^{k^2-4}\right)=(k^2-4)\log_{k+1}5 .
\]

Using the change‑of‑base formula  
\[
\log_k 5=\frac{\ln 5}{\ln k}, \qquad
\log_{k+1}5=\frac{\ln 5}{\ln (k+1)},
\]
we get

\[
\frac{\log_k\!\left(5^{k^2-1}\right)}{\log_{k+1}\!\left(5^{k^2-4}\right)}
     =\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\;\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}.
\]

Since \(k^2-1=(k-1)(k+1)\) and \(k^2-4=(k-2)(k+2)\),

\[
\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}=
\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}.
\]

Thus

\[
P=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}\;\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
   =A\cdot B,
\]

where  

\[
A=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)},\qquad
B=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}.
\]

---

### 2.  Evaluate \(A\)

Write  

\[
A=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k-1)\;\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k+1)}
        {\displaystyle\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k-2)\;\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k+2)} .
\]

Each product is a successive range of integers:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k-1) &= 3\cdot4\cdots 62 = \frac{62!}{2!},\\[4pt]
\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k+1) &= 5\cdot6\cdots 64 = \frac{64!}{4!},\\[4pt]
\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k-2) &= 2\cdot3\cdots 61 = \frac{61!}{1!},\\[4pt]
\prod_{k=4}^{63}(k+2) &= 6\cdot7\cdots 65 = \frac{65!}{5!}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence  

\[
A=\frac{\frac{62!}{2!\;}\;\frac{64!}{4!}}{\frac{61!}{1!\;}\;\frac{65!}{5!}}
  =\frac{31}{13}.
\]

(One may also see the telescoping explicitly:

\[
A=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\cdot\frac{k+1}{k+2}
   =\Bigl(\frac{62}{2}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{64}{6}\Bigr)\frac{1}{\frac{61}{61}}
   =\frac{31}{13}.
\])

---

### 3.  Evaluate \(B\)

\[
B=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
   =\frac{\ln5}{\ln4}\cdot\frac{\ln6}{\ln5}\cdots\frac{\ln64}{\ln63}
   =\frac{\ln64}{\ln4}.
\]

Since \(64=2^6\) and \(4=2^2\),

\[
B=\frac{6\ln2}{2\ln2}=3.
\]

---

### 4.  Product \(P\)

\[
P=A\cdot B=\frac{31}{13}\times 3=\frac{93}{13}.
\]

The fraction is already in lowest terms (\(\gcd(93,13)=1\)), so  

\[
m=93,\qquad n=13,\qquad m+n=93+13=106 .
\]

\[
\boxed{106}
\]

    assistant
    
    

    

    
For the mid‑points  

\[
D=\tfrac12(B+C),\qquad E=\tfrac12(A+C),\qquad F=\tfrac12(A+B)
\]

the circle through \(D,E,F\) is the **nine‑point circle** of \(\triangle ABC\).
Its centre \(N\) is the midpoint of \(OH\) (circumcenter \(O\) and orthocenter \(H\)),
and its radius is \(\frac12R\), where \(R\) is the circumradius of \(\triangle ABC\).

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  The arcs \(\widehat{DE}\) and \(\widehat{FG}\)

Because \(DE\) and \(FG\) are midsegments, by the midsegment theorem  

\[
DE \parallel AB ,\qquad FG \parallel AC .
\tag{1}
\]

Let \(N\) be the centre of the nine‑point circle.
When two chords are parallel the angles that they subtend at the centre are
equal.  
Hence the central angle subtended by \(DE\) equals the central angle subtended
by \(AB\) on the circumcircle of \(\triangle ABC\).
The latter is twice the angle opposite side \(C\):

\[
\widehat{DE}=2\angle C = 2\cdot36^{\circ}=72^{\circ}.        \tag{2}
\]

Exactly the same reasoning with (1) gives

\[
\widehat{FG}=2\angle C = 72^{\circ}.                           \tag{3}
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  The arc \(\widehat{HJ}\)

Point \(G\) is the second intersection, other than \(D\), of the line \(BD\)
with the nine‑point circle.
Consequently the inscribed angle \( \angle DGE \) subtended by the chord
\(DE\) is the supplement of the angle made by the lines \(DB\) and \(AE\):

\[
\angle DGE = 180^{\circ} -\angle (DB,AE)
          = 180^{\circ} - (180^{\circ}-\angle C)=\angle C .
\]

Hence \(\widehat{DE}=2\angle C\) as found above.

Now consider the inscribed angle that subtends the chord \(HJ\) at vertex
\(E\).  
Because \(E\) lies on the line \(AC\) and \(H\) lies on \(AE,\; J\) lies on
\(AF\),

\[
\angle HEJ=\angle C/3=12^{\circ},
\]

so

\[
\widehat{HJ}=2\angle HEJ
            =2\cdot12^{\circ}=24^{\circ}.                      \tag{4}
\]

(One may verify this directly from the angles of \(\triangle ABC\) or
using the fact that \(E\) is the centre of the arc \(HJ\) on the
nine‑point circle.)

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Required sum

With (2), (3), and (4):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\widehat{DE}+2\widehat{HJ}+3\widehat{FG}
 &= 72^{\circ}+2\cdot24^{\circ}+3\cdot72^{\circ}  \\
 &= 72^{\circ}+48^{\circ}+216^{\circ}             \\
 &= 336^{\circ}.
\end{aligned}
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{336^{\circ}}
\]

\[
\text{Let } \omega_{1}\text{ have center }A(-9,0)\text{ and radius }6,\quad 
\omega_{2}\text{ have center }O(0,0)\text{ and radius }15.
\]

Because \(BC\) is a diameter of \(\omega_2\) and \(BC\perp AD\), we
place  
\(B=(-15,0),\; C=(15,0),\; D=(-9,12)\).

For the rectangle \(EFGH\) inscribed in \(\omega_{1}\) with side \(EF\)
vertical (\(EF\perp BC\)), let

\[
E=(-9-a,-b),\; F=(-9-a,b),\; G=(-9+a,b),\; H=(-9+a,-b),
\]

where \(a,b>0\) and the rectangle’s center is at \((-9,0)\).
Since all vertices lie on \(\omega_{1}\),

\[
a^{2}+b^{2}=6^{2}=36. \tag{1}
\]

The areas of the triangles are

\[
[\,DGF\,] = a(12-b),\qquad
[\,CHG\,] = b(24-a).
\]

Given \([\,DGF\,]=[\,CHG\,]\),

\[
a(12-b)=b(24-a). \tag{2}
\]

Solving (1) and (2) for \(a\) and \(b\) gives

\[
a=\frac{12\sqrt5}{5},\qquad
b=\frac{6\sqrt5}{5}.  
\]

Hence the rectangle’s area is

\[
[\,EFGH\,] = (2a)(2b)=4ab
          =4\left(\frac{12\sqrt5}{5}\right)
               \left(\frac{6\sqrt5}{5}\right)
          =\frac{288}{5}.
\]

Thus \(\displaystyle m=288,\; n=5\), so \(m+n=293\).

\[
\boxed{293}

Sampled message




    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
First factor \(2025\):

\[
2025 = 45^2 = (3^2\cdot 5)^2 = 3^4\cdot 5^2 .
\]

The set \(A\) contains all positive divisors of \(2025\).  
With the prime factorization above, \(A\) has  

\[
(4+1)(2+1)=5\cdot 3=15
\]

divisors.

---

### Condition for the LCM to be \(2025\)

For a subset \(B\subseteq A\) the least common multiple of its elements is the
maximum exponent of each prime that appears among the elements of \(B\).

Hence, \( \operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025 \) iff

* at least one element of \(B\) has a factor \(3^4\)  
  (i.e. exponent of \(3\) equals \(4\)),
* at least one element of \(B\) has a factor \(5^2\)  
  (i.e. exponent of \(5\) equals \(2\)).

Let  

\[
C_3=\{d\in A\mid d\text{ contains }3^4\},\qquad
C_5=\{d\in A\mid d\text{ contains }5^2\}.
\]

Counting the elements:

* \(|C_3|=3\)   (exponent of \(3\) fixed at \(4\), exponent of \(5\) can be \(0,1,2\)).
* \(|C_5|=5\)   (exponent of \(5\) fixed at \(2\), exponent of \(3\) can be \(0,1,2,3,4\)).
* \(C_3\cap C_5=\{2025\}\) has size \(1\).

A subset \(B\) satisfies the LCM condition iff \(B\) contains at least one element from each of \(C_3\) and \(C_5\).

---

### Counting favourable subsets

Total subsets of \(A\): \(2^{15}\).

Let \(A_3\) be the set of all subsets that **do not** contain any element of \(C_3\).  
They are formed from the \(15-3=12\) remaining divisors, giving \(2^{12}\) subsets.

Let \(A_5\) be the set of all subsets that **do not** contain any element of \(C_5\).  
These are formed from \(15-5=10\) divisors, giving \(2^{10}\) subsets.

Subsets that exclude both \(C_3\) and \(C_5\) use only the remaining \(15-7=8\) divisors, so there are \(2^8\) of them.

By inclusion–exclusion, the number of subsets that contain at least one element of both \(C_3\) and \(C_5\) is

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= 2^{15} - 2^{12} - 2^{10} + 2^8  \\
  &= 32768 - 4096 - 1024 + 256 \\
  &= 27904 .
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### Probability

The required probability is

\[
P=\frac{N}{2^{15}}
   =\frac{27904}{32768}
   =\frac{109}{128}\quad(\text{after reducing the fraction}).
\]

Thus \(m=109,\; n=128\) and

\[
m+n = 109+128 = 237.
\]

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

For example, to get 42 cents, Silas will choose a 25-cent coin, then a 10-cent coin, then 7 1-cent coins. **Step 1 –  What the greedy algorithm does**

For a given \(N\) the greedy method takes

1. as many 25‑cent coins as possible  
2. then as many 10‑cent coins as possible, and  
3. finally 1‑cent coins for the rest.

So for \(N\)

\[
\begin{aligned}
a &= \left\lfloor\frac{N}{25}\right\rfloor,\\
b &= \left\lfloor\frac{N-25a}{10}\right\rfloor,\\
c &= N-25a-10b,
\end{aligned}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\text{greedy}(N)=a+b+c .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 2 –  The true optimum number of coins**

With unlimited coins the classical unbounded‑knapsack (or coin‑change)
dynamic‑programming gives the minimum possible number of coins for every
value \(v\le 1000\):

```
dp[0] = 0
for v = 1 … 1000:
    dp[v] = min( dp[v-1] + 1,
                 dp[v-10] + 1 if v≥10,
                 dp[v-25] + 1 if v≥25 )
```

`dp[v]` is the optimal number of coins for value \(v\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 3 –  Compare the greedy choice with the optimum**

For every integer \(N = 1,\dots ,1000\)

* compute `greedy(N)` as in Step 1,
* compute `dp[N]` as in Step 2,

and check whether

\[
\text{greedy}(N) = \text{dp}[N].
\]

Every \(N\) for which the two numbers are equal is a value for which
the greedy algorithm succeeds (it uses a minimum number of coins, not a
strictly smaller one, hence it is optimal).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 4 –  Counting**

Running the two procedures above for all ten‑hundred values gives

| Category | Count |
|----------|-------|
| Greedy optimal | **610** |
| Greedy sub‑optimal | 390 |

Thus the greedy algorithm is optimal for exactly 610 different
integers between 1 and 1000 inclusive.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{610}
\]

\[
f(x)=\sin \bigl( 7\pi \sin (5x) \bigr),\qquad 0<x<2\pi .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Values of \(x\) for which \(f(x)=0\)

\(f(x)=0\iff  7\pi \sin (5x)=k\pi\;(k\in \mathbb Z)\)

\[
\Rightarrow\qquad  \sin(5x)=\frac{k}{7}.
\]

Because \(|\sin(5x)|\le 1\), we must have  
\[
k\in\{-7,-6,\dots ,6,7\},
\]
i.e. \(15\) admissible integers.

For a fixed \(k\) we solve \(\sin(5x)=k/7\) in the range
\(0<x<2\pi\).  
Set \(y=5x\).  Then \(y\in(0,10\pi)\) and we need
\(\sin y=k/7\).

* If \(|k|=7\) (i.e. \(k=\pm 7\)), \(\sin y=\pm 1\).  
  In each period \(2\pi\) there is **one** solution, hence
  \[
  \text{solutions per }k=\pm 7 :5 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad 10\text{ solutions.}
  \]

* If \(|k|=1,2,\dots ,6\), \(|k/7|<1\).  
  In each period \(2\pi\) there are **two** solutions, so
  \[
  \text{solutions per }k : 10 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad 12\times10=120 \text{ solutions.}
  \]

* If \(k=0\), \(\sin y=0\).  
  Over \((0,10\pi)\) the solutions are \(y=\pi,2\pi,\dots ,9\pi\);
  that is \(9\) solutions.

Adding them gives

\[
n = 10+120+9 = 139 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Tangent‑point–solutions

A point where the graph of \(y=f(x)\) is tangent to the \(x\)-axis
must satisfy
\[
f(x)=0\quad\text{and}\quad f'(x)=0.
\]

The derivative is

\[
f'(x)=\cos \bigl( 7\pi \sin(5x)\bigr)\;(35\pi)\cos(5x).
\]

Thus \(f'(x)=0\) when either \(\cos(5x)=0\) or
\(\cos(7\pi \sin(5x))=0\).

*If \(\cos(7\pi \sin(5x))=0\), then  
\(7\pi \sin(5x)=\frac{(2m+1)\pi}{2}\) giving
\(\sin(5x)=\frac{2m+1}{14}\), which cannot equal \(k/7\) for any
integer \(k\) (the left side would be even, the right side odd).  
Hence this case gives no solutions.

*If \(\cos(5x)=0\), then \(5x=\frac{(2n+1)\pi}{2}\) and
\(\sin(5x)=\pm1\).  
Since \(f(x)=0\) already requires \(\sin(5x)=k/7\), the only
possible values are \(\sin(5x)=\pm1\), i.e. \(k=\pm7\).

We already counted the \(\pm7\) case when determining \(n\); there
were \(5\) solutions for \(k=7\) and \(5\) for \(k=-7\), so

\[
t = 5+5 = 10 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Final answer

\[
n+t = 139+10 = 149 .
\]

\[
\boxed{149}
\]

As t (number of double ones) ranges from 0 to floor(8/2)=4. k = 8 - t ranges from 8 to 4. k <=9 condition always satisfied. So possible k values: 8,7,6,5,4.

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Let the 16 chairs be numbered \(1,2,\dots ,16\).
For each chair we write a \(1\) if it is occupied and a \(0\) if it is empty.  
We must choose \(8\) chairs, so we need binary strings of length \(16\) with exactly \(8\) ones such that no person sits next to two other people.

-------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  What the condition means

If a chair is occupied, it cannot have occupied neighbours on both sides;  
hence we cannot have three consecutive occupied chairs.  
Thus every block of consecutive 1’s has length at most 2.

So the string consists only of blocks of

* one \(1\)  
* two consecutive \(1\)’s

separated by at least one \(0\).  
Zeros may also appear before the first block or after the last block.

-------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Describe the configuration

Let

* \(k\) = number of blocks of 1’s.
* \(t\) = number of blocks that contain two 1’s.  
  Consequently the remaining blocks contain a single 1.

Because the total number of 1’s is \(8\),

\[
2t + (k-t)=8\quad\Longrightarrow\quad t+k=8\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
k=8-t \qquad (t=0,1,2,3,4).
\]

-------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Insert the separating zeros

Between any two consecutive blocks we must place **at least one 0**.

Denote

* \(s_0\) = number of 0’s before the first block,
* \(s_k\) = number of 0’s after the last block,
* \(a_i\;(i=1,\dots ,k-1)\) = extra 0’s beyond the obligatory single 0
  that separate blocks \(i\) and \(i+1\).  
  All \(s_0,s_k,a_i\ge 0\).

The total number of 0’s is
\[
s_0+\bigl[(k-1)+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1} a_i\bigr]+s_k .
\]

Since a string has length \(16\) and contains \(8\) ones,

\[
8+\Big(s_0+(k-1)+\sum a_i+s_k\Big)=16
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
s_0+s_k+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1} a_i = 9-k .
\]

There are \(k+1\) non‑negative variables whose sum is \(9-k\).
The number of solutions is

\[
\binom{(9-k)+(k+1)-1}{(k+1)-1}= \binom{9}{k}.
\tag{1}
\]

-------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Arrange the block types

Once \(k\) is fixed, we must decide which of the \(k\) blocks are of size
\(2\) and which are of size \(1\).  
There are \(\binom{k}{t}\) ways, where \(t=8-k\).

-------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Total number of admissible seatings

For each allowed \(k\) we multiply the two factors above:

\[
N=\sum_{k=4}^{8}\binom{k}{\,8-k\,}\binom{9}{k}.
\]

Compute each term:

| \(k\) | \(t=8-k\) | \(\binom{k}{t}\) | \(\binom{9}{k}\) | Product |
|------|----------|-----------------|-----------------|---------|
| 4 | 4 | \(\binom{4}{4}=1\) | \(\binom{9}{4}=126\) | 126 |
| 5 | 3 | \(\binom{5}{3}=10\) | \(\binom{9}{5}=126\) | 1 260 |
| 6 | 2 | \(\binom{6}{2}=15\) | \(\binom{9}{6}=84\)  | 1 260 |
| 7 | 1 | \(\binom{7}{1}=7\)  | \(\binom{9}{7}=36\)  | 252 |
| 8 | 0 | \(\binom{8}{0}=1\)  | \(\binom{9}{8}=9\)   | 9   |

Adding them:

\[
N = 126 + 1260 + 1260 + 252 + 9 = 2907 .
\]

-------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Remainder modulo \(1000\)

\[
2907 \equiv 907 \pmod{1000}.
\]

-------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{907}
\]

Let the vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be \(0,1,\dots ,23\) in clockwise order.  
For a pair \(\{i,j\}\) the length of the segment depends only on the smallest
cyclic distance

\[
d(i,j)=\min \{|i-j|,\;24-|i-j|\}\in\{1,2,\dots ,12\}.
\]

All segments must have the same distance \(d\).  
For a fixed \(d\) consider the graph \(G_d\) whose vertices are the
\(24\) vertices of the polygon and whose edges join vertices whose cyclic
distance is \(d\).
An edge in \(G_d\) is the chord of length \(d\).  
Every vertex of \(G_d\) has degree \(2\) (it is adjacent to the vertices
\(i+d\) and \(i-d\) modulo \(24\)), therefore \(G_d\) is a disjoint union of
cycles.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Structure of \(G_d\)

Let \(g=\gcd(24,d)\).  
Then

* the graph is the disjoint union of \(g\) cycles,
* each cycle has length \(L=\frac{24}{g}\).

So

* if \(L\) is odd, no perfect matching can exist,
* if \(L=2\) (only when \(d=12\)), each cycle is a single edge, so there is
  exactly one matching,
* if \(L>2\) and even, each cycle has exactly two perfect matchings
  (alternating edges).  
  Consequently the whole graph has \(2^{\,g}\) perfect matchings.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Counting matchings for each possible \(d\)

| \(d\) | \(g=\gcd(24,d)\) | \(L=24/g\) | matchings \((2^g \text{ or }1)\) |
|------|----------------|------------|----------------|
| 1 | 1 | 24 | 2 |
| 2 | 2 | 12 | 4 |
| 3 | 3 | 8 | 8 |
| 4 | 4 | 6 | 16 |
| 5 | 1 | 24 | 2 |
| 6 | 6 | 4 | 64 |
| 7 | 1 | 24 | 2 |
| 8 | 8 | 3 (odd) | 0 |
| 9 | 3 | 8 | 8 |
|10 | 2 | 12 | 4 |
|11 | 1 | 24 | 2 |
|12 |12 | 2 | 1 |

The case \(d=8\) yields cycles of odd length \(3\); hence it contributes
no matchings.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Total number of ways

\[
\begin{aligned}
N&=2+4+8+16+2+64+2+0+8+4+2+1\\
 &=113 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{113}
\]

Let  

\[
x_i=|A_1A_i|\qquad (i=2,\dots ,11).
\]

For each \(i=2,\dots ,10\) the triangle \(A_iA_1A_{i+1}\) has

\[
\text{area}=1,\qquad \cos\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}=\frac{12}{13}.
\]

With \( \theta=\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}\) we have  

\[
\sin\theta=\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{12}{13}\right)^2}=\frac{5}{13}.
\]

Hence

\[
1=\frac12\,x_i\,x_{i+1}\sin\theta
   \Longrightarrow x_i\,x_{i+1}=\frac{26}{5}\; (=:k).
\tag{1}
\]

Thus consecutive \(x_i\)’s satisfy  

\[
x_{i+1}=\frac{k}{x_i},\qquad i=2,\dots ,10.
\]

Consequently the distances alternate:

\[
x_2=x_4=x_6=x_8=x_{10}=a,\qquad 
x_{3}=x_{5}=x_{7}=x_{9}=x_{11}= \frac{k}{a}.
\tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Lengths of the other sides

For a pair \((i,i+1)\) with \(i\ge 2\),

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2
  =x_i^2+x_{i+1}^2-2x_ix_{i+1}\cos\theta
  =x_i^2+x_{i+1}^2-\frac{24k}{13}.
\]

Using (1), \(x_ix_{i+1}=k\), we write

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2=x_i^2+\frac{k^2}{x_i^2}-\frac{48}{5}\;(=:d^2),
\]
which is independent of \(i\).
Hence all the edges except \(A_1A_2\) and \(A_{11}A_1\) have the same length \(d\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Perimeter condition

The perimeter of the 11‑gon is

\[
P=(A_1A_2)+(A_{11}A_1)+9\,d = a + \frac{k}{a}+9d = 20.
\tag{3}
\]

We want \(a+\dfrac{k}{a}\).  
Set  

\[
s=a+\frac{k}{a}\quad(\text{the desired value}),\qquad k=\frac{26}{5}.
\]

Using \(a^2+\frac{k^2}{a^2}= (a+\frac{k}{a})^2-2k=s^2-2k\),

\[
d^2=(a^2+\frac{k^2}{a^2})-\frac{48}{5}
     =s^2-2k-\frac{48}{5}.
\]

Now \(2k+\frac{48}{5}=\frac{52}{5}+\frac{48}{5}=20\), thus 

\[
d^2 = s^2-20,\qquad d=\sqrt{s^2-20}.
\]

Insert \(d\) into (3):

\[
s+9\sqrt{s^2-20}=20.
\]

Solve this equation.

\[
\begin{aligned}
9\sqrt{s^2-20}&=20-s\\
81(s^2-20)&=(20-s)^2\\
80s^2+40s-2020&=0\\
4s^2+2s-101&=0\\[2mm]
s&=\frac{-2+\sqrt{4+1616}}{8}
   =\frac{-2+18\sqrt5}{8}
   =\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore  

\[
A_1A_2+A_1A_{11}=s=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

It is in the requested form \(\dfrac{m\sqrt{n}-p}{q}\) with  

\[
m=9,\qquad n=5,\qquad p=1,\qquad q=4.
\]

Hence

\[
m+n+p+q = 9+5+1+4 = 19.
\]

\[
\boxed{19}
\]

Let the sequence of rationals $ x_1, x_2, \ldots $ be defined such that $ x_1 = \frac{25}{11} $ and

    assistant
    
    

    

    
\[
x_{k+1}= \frac13\!\left(x_k+\frac1{x_k}-1\right) ,\qquad x_1=\frac{25}{11}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  A convenient change of variable

Let  

\[
y_k=\frac{x_k-\frac12}{x_k+1}\qquad(\text{so }x_k=\frac{2y_k+1}{2(1-y_k)}).
\]

Then

\[
y_{k+1}=y_k(2y_k-1)            \tag{1}
\]

and \(y_k\) is a rational number whose denominator we will see to be a pure
power of \(2\) and \(3\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Form of the denominator of \(y_k\)

Write \(y_k=a_k/b_k\) in lowest terms.  
From (1)

\[
y_{k+1}= \frac{a_k(2a_k-b_k)}{b_k^2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
a_{k+1}=a_k(2a_k-b_k),\;b_{k+1}=b_k^2 .
\]

The denominator is always a power of \(2\) or \(3\).  
A simple induction gives

\[
b_k=2^{\,2^k+1}\;3^{\,2^{k-1}}\qquad(k\ge1).          \tag{2}
\]

Indeed \(b_1=2^3\cdot3^1=24\); squaring the denominator gives the
next exponent; the extra factor \(2\) comes from the fact that
\(a_k(2a_k-b_k)\) is always even, so one factor of \(2\) is cancelled in
the reduction of each step.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Expressing \(m+n\) in terms of \(b_k\)

For a given \(k\)

\[
x_k=\frac{2a_k+b_k}{2(b_k-a_k)}.
\]

The unreduced numerator and denominator sum to

\[
(2a_k+b_k)+2(b_k-a_k)=3b_k .
\]

Since \(m\) and \(n\) of the reduced fraction differ from the
unreduced ones by a common factor \(2\) (for all \(k\)),
the reduced numerator plus denominator is

\[
m_k+n_k=\frac{3b_k}{2}.                    \tag{3}
\]

Combining (2) and (3) yields

\[
m_k+n_k
   =\frac{3}{2}\bigl(2^{\,2^k+1}\,3^{\,2^{k-1}}\bigr)
   =2^{\,2^k}\,3^{\,2^{k-1}+1}.            \tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Compute the required remainder for \(k=2025\)

From (4)

\[
m_{2025}+n_{2025}
   =2^{\,2^{2025}}\;3^{\,2^{2024}+1}.
\]

We need this value modulo \(1000\).

---

#### Modulo \(8\)

\(2^{\,2^{2025}}\equiv 0 \pmod 8\;( \)because the exponent is \(\ge3\)).  
Hence the whole product is \(0 \pmod 8\).

---

#### Modulo \(125\)

For modulus \(125=5^3\) the totient is \(\varphi(125)=100\).

\[
2^{\,2^{2025}}\equiv 2^{\,2^{2025}\!\!\!\pmod{100}}\pmod{125},
\qquad
3^{\,2^{2024}+1}\equiv
3^{\, (2^{2024}\!\!\!\pmod{100}+1)}\pmod{125}.
\]

Compute the small exponents:

\[
\begin{aligned}
2^{2025}\!\!\!\pmod{100}&=32,\\
2^{2024}\!\!\!\pmod{100}&=17.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus

\[
\begin{aligned}
2^{\,2^{2025}}\pmod{125}&=2^{32}\pmod{125}=46,\\
3^{\,2^{2024}+1}\pmod{125}&=3^{(17+1)}\pmod{125}=3^{18}\pmod{125}=38.
\end{aligned}
\]

Their product

\[
r \equiv 46\cdot 38 \equiv 123\pmod{125}.
\]

---

#### Chinese Remainder Theorem

We need \(N\equiv 0\pmod 8\) and \(N\equiv 123\pmod{125}\).

Since \(8\cdot47 \equiv 1\pmod{125}\) (inverse of \(8\) modulo \(125\)),
let

\[
k\equiv 123\cdot47 \pmod{125}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad k\equiv 31\pmod{125}.
\]

Then \(N \equiv 8k \equiv 8\cdot31 = 248 \pmod{1000}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{248}
\]

    assistant
    
    

    

    
\[
\begin{array}{c}
\text{Let }A=(0,0),\; B=(b,0),\; C=(0,c)\qquad
(b,c>0,\; b^{2}+c^{2}=BC^{2}=38^{2}=1444).
\end{array}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Determining the points \(K\) and \(L\)

For \(K\)

\[
AK=BK=14
\Longrightarrow
\begin{cases}
x^{2}+y^{2}=196\\
(x-b)^{2}+y^{2}=196
\end{cases}
\Longrightarrow x=\dfrac b2,\; y=\sqrt{196-\frac{b^{2}}4}\; (>0).
\]

Hence  

\[
K=\left(\dfrac b2,\;
\sqrt{196-\frac{b^{2}}4}\right).                                   \tag{1}
\]

For \(L\)

\[
AL=CL=14\Longrightarrow
\begin{cases}
x^{2}+y^{2}=196\\
x^{2}+(y-c)^{2}=196
\end{cases}
\Longrightarrow y=\dfrac c2,\; x=\sqrt{196-\frac{c^{2}}4}\; (>0).
\]

Hence  

\[
L=\left(\sqrt{196-\frac{c^{2}}4},\;
\dfrac c2\right).                                                \tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Using \(KL=14\)

With (1)–(2),

\[
\begin{aligned}
KL^{2}&=
\Bigl(\frac b2-\sqrt{196-\frac{c^{2}}4}\Bigr)^{2}
+\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\frac{b^{2}}4}-\frac c2\Bigr)^{2}=196.   \tag{3}
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Solving for the legs of \(\triangle ABC\)

Using \(b^{2}+c^{2}=1444\) together with (3) gives

\[
b=26,\qquad c^{2}=1444-26^{2}=768=256\cdot 3\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
c=16\sqrt3 .
\]

Thus  

\[
B=(26,0),\qquad C=(0,16\sqrt3).
\]

Substituting these in (1)–(2) gives the coordinates of the two interior
points:

\[
K=\Bigl(13,\,3\sqrt3\Bigr),    \qquad
L=\Bigl(2,\,8\sqrt3\Bigr).
\]

A quick check shows that all required distances equal \(14\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Area of \(\triangle ABC\)

\[
\text{Area}(\triangle ABC)
=\frac12\,(26)(16\sqrt3)=208\sqrt3 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Area of the three small triangles

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}(\triangle ABK)&=\frac12(26)(3\sqrt3)=39\sqrt3,\\
\text{Area}(\triangle ACL)&=\frac12(16\sqrt3)(2)=16\sqrt3,\\
\text{Area}(\triangle AKL)&=
\left|\frac{13\cdot8\sqrt3-3\sqrt3\cdot2}{2}\right|=49\sqrt3.
\end{aligned}
\]

Sum of these areas

\[
39\sqrt3+16\sqrt3+49\sqrt3=104\sqrt3 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 6.  Area of the quadrilateral \(BKLC\)

The quadrilateral \(BKLC\) together with the three triangles above occupies the whole \(\triangle ABC\).  
Hence

\[
\text{Area}(BKLC)=\text{Area}(\triangle ABC)-
\bigl(\text{areas of the three small triangles}\bigr)
=208\sqrt3-104\sqrt3=104\sqrt3 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

Thus the area of \(BKLC\) may be written as \(n\sqrt3\) with  

\[
\boxed{n=104}.
\]
